Quadratic Corrections to the Higher-Spin Equations by the
Differential Homotopy Approach

Abstract
The recently proposed differential homotopy approach to the analysis of nonlinear higher spin theory is developed. The Ansatz is extended to the form applicable in the second order of the perturbation theory and general star-multiplication formulae are derived. The relation of the shifted homotopy and differential homotopy formalisms is worked out. Projectively-compact spin-local quadratic (anti)holomorphic vertices in the one-form sector of higher-spin equations are obtained within the differential homotopy formalism.



FIAN/TD/10–25

1
Introduction

1.1
General background

The theory of higher-spin (HS) fields is one of fundamental areas of
theoretical physics for many years. In 1936, Dirac in the seminal work
[
1
]
proposed equations for free HS fields later investigated by various authors including Sakata and Yukawa
[
2
]
, Fierz and Pauli
[
3
,
4
]
, Hagen and Singh
[
5
,
6
]
, and others. For instance, Fierz
[
3
]
used Dirac’s equations to determine the quantum statistics of particles of different spins. In the joint paper
[
4
]
Fierz and Pauli formulated equations for HS particles in an external electromagnetic field. They also examined the massless limits of these equations, showing that for spin 1 they reduce to Maxwell equations while for spin 2 they match the linearized Einstein equations.

Rarita and Schwinger described spin 3/2 fields in
[
7
]
,
Fradkin formulated a theory of spin 5/2 and HS fermions
[
8
]
, while Schwinger considered spin 3
[
9
]
. Massive fields of all spins
in four-dimensional space-time were described by Singh and Hagen
[
5
,
6
]
.
In 1978, Fronsdal
[
10
]
found gauge symmetry of free massless HS equations, derived the action generating these equations of all spins, and proposed a generalized Gupta program: the search for nonlinear HS theory that would reduce to the known free (linear) cases in the weak-field limit. For the massless fields the gauge symmetry serves as the main guiding principle.

In the sixties and seventies of the XX century no-go theorems had already cast doubt on the consistency of interacting HS gauge theory. Among these was Weinberg’s low-energy theorem
[
11
]
, which imposes stringent constraints on the coupling constants of soft particles. For spins greater than two, these constraints become so restrictive that the couplings must vanish, leading Weinberg to conclude that HS particles could exist but not interact at low energies. Analogously,
the Coleman-Mandula theorem
[
12
]
and its supersymmetric
Haag-Lopuszanski-Sohnius extension
[
13
]
restricted the symmetries of the
S
-matrix in the
4d
Minkowski space, ruling out Fronsdal’s gauge symmetries. Additionally, the Aragone-Deser argument
[
14
]
prohibits minimal gravitational coupling for HS fields. A detailed review of these results and their implications for HS gauge theory can be found in
[
15
]
.

Fortunately, the assumptions underlying these no-go theorems can be circumvented. Nontrivial interactions of massless HS particles with gravity and themselves
on an anti-de Sitter (AdS) background were first constructed in
[
16
,
17
]
. Crucially, available no-go arguments relied on the implicit assumption that a HS theory can be formulated in the flat Minkowski space.
AdS space, with its non-zero cosmological constant, is fundamentally curved.
A key observation was that the HS vertex in presence of gravity was non-minimal (with the number of field derivatives growing with spin), thus evading the Aragone-Deser obstruction.

In 1992, full nonlinear HS equations fulfilling Fronsdal’s program were formulated in
[
18
]
. These equations allow perturbative derivation of interaction vertices. The question of vertex locality is one of the central issues in understanding this theory. The commutation relations of the spin-
s
HS algebra generators
T_{s_{i}}
have the structure
[
19
,
20
]
,

\left[T_{s_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax T_{s_{2}}\right]\sim T_{s_{1}+s_{2}-2}+T_{s_{1}+s_{2}-4}+\dots+T_{|s_{1}-s_{2}|+2}\,.

(1.1)

As a result, introducing a spin
s>2
field necessitates an infinite tower of HS fields as was originally pointed out in
[
21
]
,
[
22
]
. This complicates the definition of locality: while local theories with finitely many fields require vertices with a finite number of derivatives, the infinite-spin case demands a more refined approach. For fields
\left\{\phi_{s_{i}}\right\}
(where
s_{i}
denotes spin), governed by equations with coefficients
a^{n_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n_{k}}
,

\sum_{k=0\mathchar 59\relax l=1}^{\infty}a^{n_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n_{k}}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax s_{l})\partial_{n_{1}}\dots\partial_{n_{k}}\phi_{s_{1}}\dots\phi_{s_{l}}=0\mathchar 59\relax

(1.2)

an interaction is called local if there exists a
K
such that
\forall k>K
, the coefficients
a^{n_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n_{k}}
vanish for any spins
s_{i}
. The space-time spin-locality further requires that for each set of spins, there exists a
K(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax s_{l})
such that
\forall k>K(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax s_{l})
, the coefficients
a^{n_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n_{k}}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax s_{l})
vanish. The definitions of spinor spin-local
[
23
]
and projectively compact
[
24
]
vertices are currently among the most useful locality concepts in HS theory.

The situation is further complicated by field redefinitions that can render seemingly non-local vertices local. Development of shifted homotopy approach
[
25
]
-
[
29
]
made it possible to achieve significant progress in the construction of lower order interacting vertices. In particular, locality required a specific shift-parameter
\beta\rightarrow-\infty
limit. Based on this limit, a new generic system of nonlinear HS holomorphic equations that leads to local vertices in all orders has been proposed by Didenko
[
30
]
(see also
[
31
]
, where all vertices at the unconstrained level for the bosonic theory in any dimension were derived). Being distinct from the original system
[
18
]
, this one is anticipated to emerge as its
\beta\rightarrow-\infty
limit, although the precise connection is yet unknown. Independently, the way of constructing local vertices in the self-dual (also referred to as chiral) theory has been proposed in
[
32
,
33
]
.

However, a question of locality of the full nonlinear system with both holomorphic and antiholomorphic sectors is still open. Extension of the shifted homotopy approach to higher orders is both not general enough and technically complicated. Its appropriate generalization is provided by the differential homotopy approach
[
34
]
, that postpones the integration over homotopy parameters to the very last step. In this approach the shift and homotopy parameters are treated as coordinates of the
space of homotopy parameters
integration over which is performed in the end. Such a generalization extends the space of solutions of the perturbative equations hence providing alternative ways for exploring locality of the HS theory. In
[
34
]
efficiency of this approach was
demonstrated in the analysis of the first-order corrections to the equations on one-forms and second-order corrections to the equations on zero-forms.

The aim of this paper is to extend this approach to the next order to derive quadratic corrections to the equations on the one-forms in the holomorphic sector of the theory. This will demand an appropriate generalization of the
differential homotopy approach to higher orders. In particular, it will be shown that the shifted homotopy approach is a particular case of the differential homotopy one. Also, we propose a modification of the Ansatz
of
[
34
]
appropriate for the second order computations and
derive new star-multiplication formulae applicable at any order.
One of the important advantages of the differential homotopy approach is that it allows one to obtain the final
results with arbitrary homotopy parameters including
\beta
introduced in
[
27
]

directly without using auxiliary statements like
Z
-dominance Lemma of
[
25
]
that was heavily used in
[
28
,
29
]
.

The interaction vertices evaluated in this paper were previously derived using shifted homotopy approach in
[
27
]
where locality was achieved in the
\beta\rightarrow-\infty
limit. In our computations the locality
is also achieved in the appropriate
\beta\rightarrow-\infty
limit, where it coincides with the result of
[
27
]
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.

After a brief introduction to HS theory and the definition of the system of equations to be solved, Section 2 describes the Ansatz used to seek second-order perturbative solutions and derives its key properties. Specifically, it provides computationally efficient formulae for the star products.

Section 3 presents all calculations leading to quadratic interaction vertices in the holomorphic sector of one-forms. A concluding discussion of the results in Section 4 is followed by technical appendices:

•

Appendix A details star-product formulae for arbitrary canonical functions.

•

Appendix B proves that any shift homotopy can be reformulated in terms of differential ones.

•

Appendix C compares
B_{2}
fields yielding projectively compact quadratic vertices in holomorphic zero-form sector via two approaches: shifted vs. differential homotopies.

•

Appendix D derives the expression for the auxiliary field
W_{2}^{(2)}
.

•

Appendix E demonstrates the
\beta\to-\infty
limiting procedure for the interaction vertex.

1.2
Higher-spin equations

A nonlinear system of equations of motion for massless fields of all spins in
AdS_{4}

studied in this paper is formulated as follows

[
18
]
.

The fields propagate in a four-dimensional space-time with local coordinates
x
.
In addition, the space of four-component spinors
Y_{A}
=
(y_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}_{\dot{\alpha}})
and
Z_{A}
=
(z_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{z}_{\dot{\alpha}})
, where
\alpha\mathchar 59\relax\dot{\alpha}\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax 2\}
, is introduced. De Rham differential is introduced both on the
x
space,
\mathrm{d}_{x}=\mathrm{d}x^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}
, and on the
Z
space,
\mathrm{d}_{Z}=\theta^{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z^{A}}
, where
\mathrm{d}x^{n}
and
\theta^{A}=\left(\theta^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\right)
are anticommuting differentials,

\{\mathrm{d}x^{m}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{d}x^{n}\}=0\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\{\theta_{A}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{B}\}=0\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\{\theta_{A}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{d}x^{n}\}=0.

(1.3)

The Klein operators
K
=
k\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\bar{k}
are defined to act as follows:

\{k\mathchar 59\relax y_{\alpha}\}=\{k\mathchar 59\relax z_{\alpha}\}=0\mathchar 59\relax\quad[k\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}_{\dot{\alpha}}]=[k\mathchar 59\relax\bar{z}_{\dot{\alpha}}]=0\mathchar 59\relax\quad k^{2}=1\mathchar 59\relax\quad[k\mathchar 59\relax\bar{k}]=0.

(1.4)

(Analogously for
\bar{k}
.) A symplectic form is defined on the spinor spaces

\epsilon_{AB}=(\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}})\mathchar 59\relax\quad\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=-\epsilon_{\beta\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=-\epsilon_{\dot{\beta}\dot{\alpha}}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\epsilon_{12}=1.

(1.5)

The fields are described in terms of one-forms
W(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)=W_{n}(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)\mathrm{d}x^{n}
,
S(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)=S_{A}(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)\theta^{A}
and zero-forms
B(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)
. Note that the variables
Z
were introduced to build interaction and the physical fields are
Z-
independent. So, physical fields are described by one-forms
\omega(Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)=W(0\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)
such that

\omega(Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K|x)=\sum_{j=0}^{1}\sum_{n=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!m!}\omega_{\alpha_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\alpha_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\dot{\alpha}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\dot{\alpha}_{m}}^{(j)}(x)y^{\alpha_{1}}\dots y^{\alpha_{n}}\bar{y}^{\dot{\alpha}_{1}}\dots\bar{y}^{\dot{\alpha}_{m}}(k\bar{k})^{j}\mathchar 59\relax

(1.6)

where the components with
n+m=2(s-1)
correspond to the spin
s
field (the components with
n=m
describe the integer spin frame-like field, while those with
n=m\pm 1
describe the half-integer spin frame-like field; the other components describe their derivatives); and zero-forms
C(Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)=B(0\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)
,

C(Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K|x)=\sum_{j=0}^{1}\sum_{n=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2n!m!}C_{\alpha_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\alpha_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\dot{\alpha}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\dot{\alpha}_{m}}^{(j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1-j)}(x)y^{\alpha_{1}}\dots y^{\alpha_{n}}\bar{y}^{\dot{\alpha}_{1}}\dots\bar{y}^{\dot{\alpha}_{m}}k^{j}\bar{k}^{1-j}\,.

(1.7)

The components with
n-m=\pm 2s
correspond to the spin
s
field (components with
n=0
or
m=0
in the case of
s=0
and
s=\frac{1}{2}
describe the
dynamical matter fields while other components are expressed in terms of derivatives of the dynamical fields). The full system of nonlinear HS equations is

[
18
]

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{x}W+W\star W=0\mathchar 59\relax

(1.8a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{x}B+W\star B-B\star W=0\mathchar 59\relax

(1.8b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{x}S+S\star W+W\star S=0\mathchar 59\relax

(1.8c)

\displaystyle S\star B-B\star S=0\mathchar 59\relax

(1.8d)

\displaystyle S\star S=i(\theta^{\alpha}\theta_{\alpha}+\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}+B\star(\eta\gamma+\bar{\eta}\bar{\gamma}))\,\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(1.8e)

where
\eta
is a complex parameter, the star-product operation
\star
is introduced as follows:

f(Z\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}Y)\star g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y)=\int f(Z+S\mathchar 59\relax Y+S)g(Z-T\mathchar 59\relax Y+T)e^{iS_{A}T^{A}}\;\mathrm{d}^{4}S\;\mathrm{d}^{4}T

(1.9)

and

\begin{gathered}\gamma=e^{iz_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}}\theta^{\alpha}\theta_{\alpha}k\mathchar 59\relax\quad f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y|\theta)\star\gamma=\gamma\star f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y|\theta)\mathchar 59\relax\\
\bar{\gamma}=e^{i\bar{z}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{y}^{\dot{\alpha}}}\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{k}\mathchar 59\relax\quad f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y|\theta)\star\bar{\gamma}=\bar{\gamma}\star f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y|\theta).\end{gathered}

(1.10)

Throughout this paper, we adopt the following normalization convention for the spinor variable integration measure:

\mathrm{d}^{2}s:=\frac{\mathrm{d}s^{1}\;\mathrm{d}s^{2}}{2\pi}\mathchar 59\relax

(1.11)

which leads, for example, to

\int\mathrm{d}^{2}s\;\mathrm{d}^{2}t\;e^{is_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}}=1.

(1.12)

The analysis of the system (
1.8a
)-(
1.8e
) is performed perturbatively. The vacuum solution is

B_{0}=0\mathchar 59\relax\quad S_{0}=\theta^{\alpha}z_{\alpha}+\bar{\theta}^{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{z}_{\dot{\alpha}}.

(1.13)

A remarkable feature of
S_{0}
is that

[S_{0}\mathchar 59\relax f(Z)]_{\star}=-2i\mathrm{d}_{Z}f(Z).

(1.14)

Substitution of (
1.13
) into equations gives
W_{0}=\omega(Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K|x)\mathchar 59\relax B_{1}=C(Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K|x)
. Further analysis reproduces the complete system of nonlinear equations for the physical HS fields:

\mathrm{d}_{x}\omega+\omega\star\omega=\Upsilon(\omega\omega C)+\Upsilon(\omega\omega CC)+\dots

(1.15)

\mathrm{d}_{x}C+\omega\star C-C\star\omega=\Upsilon(\omega CC)+\Upsilon(\omega CCC)+\dots

(1.16)

within the expansion in powers of
C
.

The consistency of the full system along with the property (
1.14
) imply that the equations, that contain
S
, have the form

\mathrm{d}_{Z}f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)

(1.17)

with some
\mathrm{d}_{Z}
closed
g
,

\mathrm{d}_{Z}g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=0\,.

(1.18)

The general solution to such equation has the form

f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=\triangle_{0}g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)+\mathrm{d}_{Z}\epsilon+h\mathchar 59\relax

(1.19)

where
h
belongs to
\mathrm{d}_{Z}
-cohomology and

\triangle_{0}g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=Z^{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{A}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}g(tZ\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax t\theta).

(1.20)

The choice of different
\mathrm{d}_{Z}
-cohomology representatives
h
leads to different interaction vertices in (
1.15
) and (
1.16
). The zero-dimensional cohomology consists of functions independent of
Z
and thus depending only on
Y
, while the first cohomology is trivial. That is why differences in the choice of
h
correspond to field redefinitions. It turns out that some solutions lead to vertices with an infinite number of derivatives over spinor variables
Y
, which eventually leads to an infinite number of derivatives over the space-time variables.
(See,
e.g.
,
[
34
]
.)

This raised a problem of developing an approach that allows one to find solutions to equations of the form (
1.17
), leading to spin-local vertices. To this end the so-called shifted homotopy method was developed in
[
25
,
26
]
, in which the solution
to the equation (
1.17
) was presented in the form

\triangle_{a}g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=\int_{0}^{1}(Z+a)^{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}g(tZ-(1-t)a\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax t\theta)\mathchar 59\relax

(1.21)

where
a
is any
Z-
independent shift. The approach was further developed with the introduction of the
\beta
-parameter
[
27
,
28
]
, leading to the construction of spin-local vertices in lower orders of the perturbation theory

[
26
,
27
,
29
]
.
However, a generalization
of these results to higher orders was not straightforward. In particular, it was speculated that the analysis would simplify the selection of shift parameters at a certain step if they would depend on the parameters such as the integration variable
t
of the previous steps, which is not feasible in the shifted homotopy approach since the integration has already been performed.

2
Differential homotopy

2.1
The space of homotopy parameters

In the differential homotopy approach of
[
34
]
, the shift parameters
a
and integration variables like
t
in (
1.21
) are treated as coordinates of some space
M=\{h^{a}\}
. This space comprises two parts:

1.

A spinorial part, whose coordinates are spinors.

2.

A homotopy part, whose coordinates are scalar homotopy parameters.

Crucially, the homotopy part must be compact to ensure the convergence of relevant expressions. Moreover, in practice, this homotopy part turns out to be contractible. Note, that integration variables
s
and
t
in (
1.9
) now belong to
M
, which means integrals over them are preserved until the final stage of computations. For this reason, we will employ the integral kernel of the previously introduced star product (
1.9
)

f(Z\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}Y)*g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y)=f(Z+S\mathchar 59\relax Y+S)g(Z-T\mathchar 59\relax Y+T)e^{iS_{A}T^{A}}\;\mathrm{d}^{4}S\;\mathrm{d}^{4}T

(2.1)

and use (
2.1
) instead of (
1.9
) in our computations.

The differential
\mathrm{d}_{h}=\mathrm{d}h^{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial h^{a}}
in
M=\{h^{a}\}
anticommutes with
\theta^{A}
. The total differential is

\mathrm{d}=\mathrm{d}_{Z}+\mathrm{d}_{h}.

(2.2)

Since
h^{a}
belong to a contractible space, all closed
h-
differential forms of positive degrees whose integral vanishes are exact. Closed zero-forms are

h^{a}
independent functions.

Equations (
1.17
) are generalized to

\mathrm{d}f(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax h\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{d}h)=g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax h\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{d}h)\mathchar 59\relax

(2.3)

with the consistency condition

\mathrm{d}g(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax h\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{d}h)=0.

(2.4)

The integral over
M
has the following property

\int_{M}\equiv\int_{h^{1}}\dots\int_{h^{k}}\equiv\int_{h^{1}\dots h^{k}}=\mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\int_{\sigma(h^{1})\dots\sigma(h^{k})}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.5)

where
\sigma
is a permutation of
k
symbols. Additionally, the order of integral signs and differentials can be arranged arbitrarily with the commutation rules

\mathrm{d}\int_{h^{1}}=-\int_{h^{1}}\mathrm{d}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathrm{d}\int_{h^{1}\dots h^{k}}=(-1)^{k}\int_{h^{1}\dots h^{k}}\mathrm{d}\,.

(2.6)

Also, it should be stressed that the integral of a differential form is nonzero only if its degree coincides with the dimension of the integration cycle. This will play an important role in the sequel.

2.2
Weak terms

A differential form
F(Z\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}h)
, understood to have implicitly assumed dependence on
\theta
and
\mathrm{d}h
, is called
weak
iff

\int_{\{h\}}F=0\mathchar 59\relax

(2.7)

where {h} represents the space of homotopy parameters and additional spinor variables.
We write
F\cong G
when
F
and
G
differ by a weak term, meaning their integrals over
{h}
coincide.
It is easy to see that weak terms are closed under total differential and star products. Indeed, if a form
F
is weak (
i.e.
, satisfies (
2.7
)), then its derivative
\mathrm{d}F
is also weak by the Stockes’ theorem,

\int_{\{h\}}\mathrm{d}_{h}F=0\,\mathchar 59\relax

\int_{\{h\}}\mathrm{d}F=\int_{\{h\}}(\mathrm{d}_{Z}F+\mathrm{d}_{h}F)=\int_{\{h\}}\mathrm{d}_{Z}F=(-1)^{|\{h\}|}\mathrm{d}_{Z}\int_{\{h\}}F=0\,\mathchar 59\relax

(2.8)

where
|\{h\}|
counts the number of integration variables
h^{a}
.

Analogously, given a weak form
F(Z\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}h_{1})
and an arbitrary form
G(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax h_{2})
such that its homotopy parameters
\{h_{2}^{a}\}_{a}
do not contain any of
h_{1}^{a}
, their star product
F*G
is weak. This follows from

\int_{\{h_{1}\}}\int_{\{h_{2}\}}\int_{ST}(F*G)(Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax h_{1}\mathchar 59\relax h_{2})=\\
=\int_{\{h_{1}\}}\int_{\{h_{2}\}}\int_{ST}\mathrm{d}^{4}S\;\mathrm{d}^{4}T\;F(Z+S\mathchar 59\relax Y+S\mathchar 59\relax h_{1})G(Z-T\mathchar 59\relax Y+T\mathchar 59\relax h_{2})e^{iS_{A}T^{A}}=\\
=(-1)^{|\{h_{1}\}|\cdot|\{h_{2}\}|}\int_{\{h_{2}\}}\int_{ST}\mathrm{d}^{4}S\;\mathrm{d}^{4}T\;\left(\int_{\{h_{1}\}}F(Z+S\mathchar 59\relax Y+S\mathchar 59\relax h_{1})\right)G(Z-T\mathchar 59\relax Y+T\mathchar 59\relax h_{2})e^{iS_{A}T^{A}}=0\mathchar 59\relax

(2.9)

where
|\{h_{i}\}|
is the number of parameters in the
i
-th group.

Also, let us notice that there are some terms that can be easily identified as weak. At least they are the ones
which represent the properties of a standard star product. For example, a star commutator of a form
F
with central element
\gamma
is weak, just like the associator of any three forms

[F\mathchar 59\relax\gamma]_{*}=\left(F*\gamma-\gamma*F\right)\cong 0;

(2.10)

(F*G)*H-F*(G*H)\cong 0.

(2.11)

Previously, the
l.h.s.
of (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) were identically zero, but now they vanish only in the weak sense,
as the equality requires integration over auxiliary spinor variables
S\mathchar 59\relax T
to be satisfied.

2.3
Fundamental Ansatz

As shown in
[
34
]
(see also text around (
3.12
)),
before solving equation for
S_{2}
, it is convenient to represent all encountered functions, starting from
S_{1}
, in the form

f\equiv f(g_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n})=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{pruv\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{l}}pruv\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{l}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}\mu(\tau\mathchar 59\relax\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{l})\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})k\mathchar 59\relax

(2.12)

where
l\in\overline{1\mathchar 59\relax n}
,
g_{l}
are functions of spinor variables
r^{\alpha}_{l}
(e.g.,
C(r)
or
\omega(r)
) with implicit dependence on
\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and space-time coordinates
x
;

\tau\mathchar 59\relax\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta
and
\sigma^{l}
are integration parameters restricted to a compact domain by the measure factor of
\mu(\tau\mathchar 59\relax\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{l})
,

\mu_{0}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\mathrm{d}^{2}p_{i}\mathrm{d}^{2}r_{i})\mathrm{d}^{2}u\mathrm{d}^{2}v\,\mathchar 59\relax

(2.13)

\begin{gathered}\Omega^{\alpha}=\tau z^{\alpha}-(1-\tau)(p^{\alpha}(\sigma)-\beta v^{\alpha}+\rho(y^{\alpha}+p_{+}^{\alpha}+u^{\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\end{gathered}

(2.14)

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{i}^{2}=\mathrm{d}\Omega_{i}^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{i\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.15)

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{E}}({\Omega})=\exp i\left({\Omega}_{\alpha}(y+p_{+}+u)^{\alpha}+u_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\right)\mathchar 59\relax\end{gathered}

(2.16)

p_{+}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\quad p(\sigma)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.17)

\bar{*}
denotes the
*
-product with respect to antiholomorphic spinor variables
\bar{y}_{\dot{\alpha}}
on which
g_{l}
depend. Note that, following
[
34
]
, we use the integral symbol solely to control the order of integration variables, where a change in order (equivalent to choosing the orientation of
M
) determines the sign of an expression. Integration itself is performed only at the last step when the interaction vertex is calculated.

Functions of the form (
2.12
) will be referred to as having canonical form or simply
canonical
.

2.3.1
Star product of two canonical functions

When solving the equation for
S_{2}
, one encounters a star product of the expressions of the form (
2.12
). Hence it is useful to have a formula for such a product. It has the form

f_{1}(g_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n})*f_{2}(g_{n+1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n+m})=\\
=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{pruvst\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{k}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{j}}pruvst\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{k}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{j}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}^{(12)}\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{n+m}-(1-\beta_{1}))\dots\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{n+1}-(1-\beta_{1}))\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{n}-(1-\beta_{2}))\dots\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{1}-(1-\beta_{2}))\times\\
\times\mu_{1}(\tau_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{l})\mu_{2}(\tau_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax-\sigma_{2}^{l})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n+m}(r_{n+m})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.18)

where

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{1}^{\alpha}=\tau_{1}z^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{1})(p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})+(1-\beta_{1})s^{\alpha}-\beta_{1}v_{1}^{\alpha}+\rho_{1}(y^{\alpha}+p_{+}^{\alpha}+s^{\alpha}+u_{1}^{\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\\
\Omega_{2}^{\alpha}=\tau_{2}z^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{2})(p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})-(1-\beta_{2})t^{\alpha}-\beta_{2}v_{2}^{\alpha}+\rho_{2}(y^{\alpha}-p_{+}^{\alpha}+t^{\alpha}+u_{2}^{\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\end{gathered}

(2.19)

{\mathcal{E}}({\Omega}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax{\Omega}_{2})=\exp i\left({\Omega}_{1\alpha}(y+s+p_{+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+{\Omega}_{2\alpha}(y+t-p_{+}+u_{2})^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+\right.\\
\left.+(s-t+p_{+})_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(2.20)

\quad p_{+\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}p_{i\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\mu_{0}^{(12)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n+m}(\mathrm{d}^{2}p_{i}\mathrm{d}^{2}r_{i})\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{2}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{2}\mathrm{d}^{2}s\mathrm{d}^{2}t\mathchar 59\relax

(2.21)

\text{and}\quad\mathbb{D}(x)=\delta(x)\mathrm{d}x.

(2.22)

We point out that while
\Omega_{1}
in
f_{1}
initially depended only on
\{\sigma_{1}^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
the remaining variables
\{\sigma_{1}^{i}\}_{i>n}
were absent in
f_{1}
. However, these
variables appear in
\Omega_{1}
when considering the star product
f_{1}*f_{2}
in
(
2.18
), where they all become expressed in terms of
1-\beta_{1}
. An analogous situation occurs for
\{\sigma_{2}^{i}\}_{i\leq n}

in
\Omega_{2}
.

Equality (
2.18
) can be obtained as follows. Consider
f_{1}(g_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n})*f_{2}(g_{n+1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n+m})
. The labels 1 and 2 refer to
\Omega
,
\sigma
,
\beta
,
\rho
,
\tau
and
p
,
u
,
v
from
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, respectively.
Straightforward application of the star product (
2.1
) yields

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{1\alpha}\rightarrow\tau_{1}(z_{\alpha}+s_{\alpha})-(1-\tau_{1})(p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})-\beta_{1}v_{1\alpha}+\rho_{1}(y_{\alpha}+p_{(1)+\alpha}+s_{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}))=\Omega_{1\alpha}^{\prime}+s_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\\
\Omega_{2\alpha}\rightarrow\tau_{2}(z_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha})-(1-\tau_{2})(p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})-\beta_{2}v_{2\alpha}+\rho_{2}(y_{\alpha}-p_{(2)+\alpha}+t_{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}))=\Omega_{2\alpha}^{\prime}-t_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\end{gathered}

(2.23)

where

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{1\alpha}^{\prime}=\tau_{1}z_{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{1})(p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})+s_{\alpha}-\beta_{1}v_{1\alpha}+\rho_{1}(y_{\alpha}+p_{(1)+\alpha}+s_{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\\
\Omega_{2\alpha}^{\prime}=\tau_{2}z_{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{2})(p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})-t_{\alpha}-\beta_{2}v_{2\alpha}+\rho_{2}(y_{\alpha}-p_{(2)+\alpha}+t_{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}));\end{gathered}

(2.24)

{\mathcal{E}}({\Omega})\rightarrow\exp i\big[(\Omega_{1\alpha}^{\prime}+s_{\alpha})(y+s+p_{(1)+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+(\Omega_{2\alpha}^{\prime}-t_{\alpha})(y+t-p_{(2)+}+u_{2})^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+\\
+u_{1{\alpha}}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2{\alpha}}v_{2}^{\alpha}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{n<i<j\leq n+m}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}p_{i{\alpha}}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big]=\\
=\exp i\big[\Omega_{1\alpha}^{\prime}(y+s+p_{(1)+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+\Omega_{2\alpha}^{\prime}(y+t-p_{(2)+}+u_{2})^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}p_{(1)+}^{\alpha}+t_{\alpha}p_{(2)+}^{\alpha}+\\
+(s-t)_{\alpha}y^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+u_{1{\alpha}}(v_{1}-s)^{\alpha}+u_{2{\alpha}}(v_{2}+t)^{\alpha}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{n<i<j\leq n+m}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}p_{i{\alpha}}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big]\mathchar 59\relax

(2.25)

where
p_{(1)+\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i{\alpha}}\mathchar 59\relax\quad p_{(2)+\alpha}=\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+m}p_{i{\alpha}}
.
The shifts
v_{1\alpha}\rightarrow v_{1\alpha}+s_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax v_{2\alpha}\rightarrow v_{2\alpha}-t_{\alpha}
and then
s_{\alpha}\rightarrow s_{\alpha}+p_{(2)+\alpha}
and
t_{\alpha}\rightarrow t_{\alpha}-p_{(1)+\alpha}
yield (
2.18
).

2.3.2
Star product of any number of canonical functions

Remarkably, (
2.18
) can be generalized to the star product of any number of canonical product factors

F_{J}:=f_{1}(g_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{1\mathchar 59\relax n_{1}})*\ldots*f_{J}(g_{J\mathchar 59\relax 1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{J\mathchar 59\relax n_{J}})

(2.26)

with

f_{j}(g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{j\mathchar 59\relax n_{j}})=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{p_{j}r_{j}u_{j}v_{j}\tau_{j}\rho_{j}\beta_{j}\sigma_{j}}p_{j}r_{j}u_{j}v_{j}\tau_{j}\rho_{j}\beta_{j}\sigma_{j}}$\hss}}}\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}p_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}r_{j}\mu_{j}(\tau_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{j})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{j}^{2}{{\mathcal{E}}(\Omega_{j})}g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}(r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1})\overline{*}\dots\overline{*}g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{j}}(r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{j}})k\,\mathchar 59\relax

(2.27)

{{\mathcal{E}}(\Omega_{j})}=\exp i\left({\Omega}_{j\alpha}(\tilde{q}_{j}+u_{j})^{\alpha}+u_{j\alpha}v_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}}p_{j\mathchar 59\relax i\alpha}r_{j\mathchar 59\relax i}^{\alpha}-\sum_{1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq n_{j}}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i\alpha}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(2.28)

\tilde{q}_{j}^{\alpha}:=y^{\alpha}+p_{(j)+}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad p_{(j)+}^{\alpha}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.29)

{\Omega}_{j\alpha}=\tau_{j}z_{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{j})(p_{j\alpha}(\sigma_{j})-\beta_{j}v_{j\alpha}+\rho_{j}(\tilde{q}_{j\alpha}+u_{j\alpha}))

(2.30)

(no summation over
j
).

Introducing the integration variables
s_{i}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax t_{i}^{\alpha}
associated with
the star products one obtains (for the proof see Appendix
A.1
)

F_{J}=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{pruvst\tau\rho\beta\sigma}pruvst\tau\rho\beta\sigma}$\hss}}}\delta^{2}(t_{1})\delta^{2}(s_{J})\mathcal{E}_{J}\prod_{j=1}^{J}\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}p_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}r_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}s_{j}\mathrm{d}^{2}t_{j}\mu_{j}(\tau_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{j})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\Omega}_{j}^{2}\\
g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}(r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1})\overline{*}\dots\overline{*}g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{j}}(r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{j}})k^{J}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.31)

where

\mathcal{E}_{J}=\exp i\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left[(s_{j\alpha}+s_{j-1\alpha})t_{j}^{\alpha}+(s_{j\alpha}-t_{j\alpha})(q_{j}^{\alpha}+u_{j}^{\alpha})+u_{j\alpha}v_{j}^{\alpha}+\vphantom{\sum_{i}}\right.\\
+\tilde{\Omega}_{j\alpha}(q_{j}^{\alpha}+u_{j}^{\alpha}+s_{j\alpha}+t_{j\alpha})-\left.\sum_{i}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i\alpha}r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<i^{\prime}}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i\alpha}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

(2.32)

\tilde{\Omega}_{j\alpha}=\tau_{j}z_{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{j})((-1)^{j+1}p_{j\alpha}(\sigma_{j})-\beta_{j}v_{j\alpha}+s_{j\alpha}-t_{j\alpha}+\rho_{j}(q_{j\alpha}+u_{j\alpha}+s_{j\alpha}+t_{j\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax

(2.33)

q_{j}^{\alpha}:=y^{\alpha}+(-1)^{j+1}p_{(j)+}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.34)

and
\prod
also implies
\bar{*}
-product between groups
g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}(r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1})\overline{*}\dots\overline{*}g_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{j}}(r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{j}})
with consecutive
j
’s.
(Here and in the sequel, objects with indices beyond the prescribed range are assumed to be zero.)

It is important that the homotopy parameters
\tau_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{j}
and
\beta_{j}
enter (
2.31
) only via
\Omega_{j}
thus
preserving the fundamental property that
\mathrm{d}
acts only on the measure for each product factor.

In the
J=2
case one can shift
v_{1\alpha}\rightarrow v_{1}^{\alpha}+s_{1}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax v_{2}^{\alpha}\rightarrow v_{2}^{\alpha}-t_{2}^{\alpha}
,
s_{1}^{\alpha}\rightarrow s_{1}^{\alpha}+p_{(2)+}^{\alpha}
,
t_{2}^{\alpha}\rightarrow t_{2}^{\alpha}-p_{(1)+}^{\alpha}
bringing (
2.31
) to (
2.18
).

As shown in Appendix
A.2
, (
2.32
), (
2.33
) can be reduced to the following form

\mathcal{E}_{J}=\exp i\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left[(s_{j\alpha}+s_{j-1\alpha})t_{j}^{\alpha}+\tilde{\Omega}_{j\alpha}(s_{j}^{\alpha}+t_{j}^{\alpha})-|J+1|_{2}\sum_{j=1}^{J}y_{\alpha}p_{(j)+}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i\alpha}r_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i}^{\alpha}\right]-\\
-\sum_{j<j^{\prime}}p_{(j)+\alpha}p_{(j^{\prime})+}^{\alpha}-\sum_{j}\sum_{i<i^{\prime}}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i\alpha}p_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i^{\prime}}^{\alpha}-v_{k\alpha}v_{k+1}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.35)

\tilde{\Omega}_{i}^{\alpha}=\tau_{i}z^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{i})\Big[(-1)^{i+1}p_{i}^{\alpha}(\sigma_{i})-\beta_{i}\big((\delta_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k}+\delta_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}k+1})v_{i}^{\alpha}+(-1)^{i}(\tilde{n}_{i}^{\alpha}-n_{i}^{\alpha})+c_{i}(s_{i}^{\alpha}+t_{i+1}^{\alpha})-b_{i}(t_{i}^{\alpha}+s_{i-1}^{\alpha})\big)+\\
+s_{i}^{\alpha}-t_{i}^{\alpha}+(-1)^{i}(\tilde{n}_{i}^{\alpha}-n_{i}^{\alpha})+(c_{i}-b_{i})q_{i}^{\alpha}+\rho_{i}(s_{i}^{\alpha}+t_{i}^{\alpha}+(-1)^{i}n_{J}^{\alpha})\Big]\mathchar 59\relax

(2.36)

where
\delta_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}
is the Kronecker delta

\delta_{i\mathchar 59\relax j}=\begin{cases}1\mathchar 59\relax&i=j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
0\mathchar 59\relax&i\neq j\mathchar 59\relax\end{cases}\qquad|n|_{2}=\begin{cases}0\mathchar 59\relax&\text{ when $n$ is even}\\
1\mathchar 59\relax&\text{ when $n$ is odd}\end{cases}

(2.37)

and

c_{i}=\begin{cases}1\mathchar 59\relax&i\leq k\mathchar 59\relax\\
0\mathchar 59\relax&i>k\mathchar 59\relax\end{cases}\qquad b_{i}=\begin{cases}0\mathchar 59\relax&i\leq k\mathchar 59\relax\\
1\mathchar 59\relax&i>k\end{cases}

(2.38)

with some
1\leq k<J
,

n_{i}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{l=1}^{i}(-1)^{l}q_{l}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\tilde{n}_{j}^{\alpha}=n_{J}^{\alpha}-n_{j-1}^{\alpha}\,.

(2.39)

Let us stress that, since the dependence on
k
resulted from
a particular change of variables (
A.14
), (
2.38
), it cannot affect the final result.
This implies that the dependence on
k
in
\tilde{\Omega}_{i}
(
2.36
) is a kind of
artificial and can be chosen in any convenient way.

2.3.3
Second-order Ansatz

To solve equations in the second order starting from
S_{2}
it is convenient to introduce a generalized second-order Ansatz, that results from the multiplication (
2.18
) of two canonical functions (
2.12
), followed by the replacement of
z^{\alpha}
by
\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}
along with introducing
u_{12}^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax v_{12}^{\alpha}
:

g\equiv g(g_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n})=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{p_{l}r_{l}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{i}\rho_{j}\beta_{i}\sigma_{i}^{l}a_{i}}p_{l}r_{l}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{i}\rho_{j}\beta_{i}\sigma_{i}^{l}a_{i}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}\mu(\tau_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{i}^{l}\mathchar 59\relax a_{i})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})\,\mathchar 59\relax

(2.40)

with
l\in\overline{1\mathchar 59\relax n}\mathchar 59\relax i\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax 12\}\mathchar 59\relax j\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax 2\}
and

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{1}^{\alpha}=\tau_{1}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{1})(p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})+a_{1}s^{\alpha}-\beta_{1}v_{1}^{\alpha}+\rho_{1}(y^{\alpha}+p_{+}^{\alpha}+s^{\alpha}+u_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{12}^{\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\\
\Omega_{2}^{\alpha}=\tau_{2}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{2})(p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})-a_{2}t^{\alpha}-\beta_{2}v_{2}^{\alpha}+\rho_{2}(y^{\alpha}-p_{+}^{\alpha}+t^{\alpha}+u_{2}^{\alpha}+u_{12}^{\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\end{gathered}

(2.41)

\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}=\tau_{12}z^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{12})(p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{12})-\beta_{12}v_{12}^{\alpha})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.42)

\mu_{0}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\mathrm{d}^{2}p_{i}\mathrm{d}^{2}r_{i})\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{2}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{2}\mathrm{d}^{2}s\mathrm{d}^{2}t\mathrm{d}^{2}u_{12}\mathrm{d}^{2}v_{12}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.43)

{\mathcal{E}}({\Omega}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax{\Omega}_{2})=\exp i\Big({\Omega}_{1\alpha}(y+s+p_{+}+u_{1}+u_{12})^{\alpha}+{\Omega}_{2\alpha}(y+t-p_{+}+u_{2}+u_{12})^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+\\
+(s-t+p_{+})_{\alpha}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}+u_{12\alpha}v_{12}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\Big).

(2.44)

In the cases where the integration over
a_{i}
is not specified we will set
a_{i}=1-\beta_{i}
.

By virtue of Taylor expansion

g_{k}(x)=e^{x^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial r^{\alpha}}}g_{k}(r)|_{r=0}
,
the
p_{k}
and
r_{k}
integrations can be replaced by a differential realization

p_{k\alpha}=-i\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{k}^{\alpha}}

(2.45)

followed by the substitution
r_{k}^{\alpha}=0
.

The expressions (
2.12
) and (
2.40
) share the following remarkable property. Since the index
\alpha
takes two values and
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\alpha}
is a one-form,
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\beta}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\gamma}=0
. Therefore, the action of
\mathrm{d}
on
\mathcal{E}
preceded by
\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}
does not contribute,
i.e.

\mathrm{d}
only acts on the measure factors like
\mu
.

2.4
Operations

In this section, we consider the operations with (
2.40
), which will be used below to solve the system of equations for HS fields. Formulae obtained below generalize those of
[
34
]
but they do not boil down to direct application of
[
34
]
along with multiplication (
2.18
) due to the presence of
\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}
(
2.42
).

Firstly consider the replacement of the factor of
g_{k}
in (
2.40
)
by
g_{k}*f
, where
f
is a
z_{\alpha}
-independent function of
y_{\alpha}
,

\int_{p_{k}r_{k}}e^{-ip_{k\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha}}(g_{k}*f)(r_{k})=\int_{pruv}g(r_{k}+u)\bar{*}f(r_{k}+v)e^{i(u_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}-p_{k\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha})}\,.

(2.46)

In the integral we change
v_{\alpha}
to
v_{\alpha}-r_{k\alpha}
, then
r_{k\alpha}
to
r_{k\alpha}-u_{\alpha}
. After that
v
should be renamed as
r_{f}
and
u
— as
-p_{f}
. This yields

\int_{p_{k}r_{k}}e^{ip_{k\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha}}(g_{k}*f)(r_{k})=\int_{p_{k}r_{k}p_{f}r_{f}}g(r_{k})\bar{*}f(r_{f})e^{i(-p_{f\alpha}r_{f}^{\alpha}+p_{f\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha}-p_{k\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha}-p_{k\alpha}p_{f}^{\alpha})}.

(2.47)

Now change
p_{k\alpha}
to
p_{f\alpha}+p_{k\alpha}
to obtain

\int_{p_{k}r_{k}}e^{ip_{k\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha}}(g_{k}*f)(r_{k})=\int_{p_{k}r_{k}p_{f}r_{f}}g(r_{k})\bar{*}f(r_{f})e^{i(-p_{f\alpha}r_{f}^{\alpha}-p_{k\alpha}r_{k}^{\alpha}-p_{k\alpha}p_{f}^{\alpha})}

(2.48)

in accordance with the Ansatz (
2.40
). Hence, the star-multiplication of one of the functions
g_{k}
by a function
f(y)
boils down to adding integration over a new parameters
\sigma_{i}^{f}
that enters the integral through
\Omega_{i}
and with
\delta(\sigma_{i}^{f}-\sigma_{i}^{k})
(
i\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax 12\}
) since
p_{k\alpha}
was replaced by
p_{f\alpha}+p_{k\alpha}
,

g(g_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{k}*f\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n})=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{p_{i}r_{i}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}\sigma_{1}^{f}\sigma_{2}^{f}\sigma_{12}^{f}}p_{i}r_{i}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}\sigma_{1}^{f}\sigma_{2}^{f}\sigma_{12}^{f}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}\mu(\tau_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{j}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax a_{i})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{f}-\sigma_{12}^{k})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{f}-\sigma_{2}^{k})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{f}-\sigma_{1}^{k})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\times\\
\times\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g(r_{k})\bar{*}f(r_{f})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n}).

(2.49)

Analogously, one can obtain

g(g_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax f*g_{k}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n})=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{p_{i}r_{i}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}\sigma_{1}^{f}\sigma_{2}^{f}\sigma_{12}^{f}}p_{i}r_{i}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}\sigma_{1}^{f}\sigma_{2}^{f}\sigma_{12}^{f}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}\mu(\tau_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{j}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax a_{i})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{f}-\sigma_{12}^{k})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{f}-\sigma_{2}^{k})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{f}-\sigma_{1}^{k})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\times\\
\times\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}f(r_{f})\bar{*}g(r_{k})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n}).

(2.50)

The next operation is the star-multiplication of an expression of the form (
2.40
) by some
z_{\alpha}
-independent function
\phi(y)
.
It is not hard to see that

g*\phi(y)=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{pruv\tau\rho\beta\sigma a\sigma_{1}^{\phi}\sigma_{2}^{\phi}\sigma_{12}^{\phi}}pruv\tau\rho\beta\sigma a\sigma_{1}^{\phi}\sigma_{2}^{\phi}\sigma_{12}^{\phi}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}\mu(\tau\mathchar 59\relax\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta\mathchar 59\relax\sigma\mathchar 59\relax a)\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{\phi}-2a_{1}+(1-\beta_{1}))\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{\phi}+(1-\beta_{2}))\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{\phi}+1-\beta_{12})\times\\
\times\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})\bar{*}\phi(r_{\phi})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.51)

\phi(y)*g=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{p_{i}r_{i}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}}p_{i}r_{i}u_{i}v_{i}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}}$\hss}}}\mu_{0}\mu(\tau\mathchar 59\relax\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta\mathchar 59\relax\sigma\mathchar 59\relax a)\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{\phi}+1-\beta_{12})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{\phi}-2a_{2}+(1-\beta_{2}))\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{\phi}+(1-\beta_{1}))\times\\
\times\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})\phi(r_{\phi})\bar{*}g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.52)

where
p_{\phi}
and
r_{\phi}
appear in (
2.41
)-(
2.44
) as
p_{i}
with
i=\phi
.

2.5
Symmetry property

There is a symmetry between the indices 1 and 2 in presence of
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{1})
or
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{2})
in (
2.40
). Namely, the following equality holds true (recall that objects with label
"12"
belong to
\Omega^{\alpha}_{12}
(
2.42
)):

\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{k}\tau_{12}a_{2}\sigma_{12}^{i}\beta_{12}}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{k}\tau_{12}a_{2}\sigma_{12}^{i}\beta_{12}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(a_{2}-(1-\beta_{2}))\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{1})\mu(\sigma_{12}^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{12}\mathchar 59\relax\tau_{12}\mathchar 59\relax\tau_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{2}^{k})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})=\\
=-\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{2}\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{k}\tau_{12}a_{1}\sigma_{12}^{i}\beta_{12}}\tau_{2}\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{k}\tau_{12}a_{1}\sigma_{12}^{i}\beta_{12}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(a_{1}-(1-\beta_{1}))\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{2})\mu(\sigma_{12}^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{12}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\tau_{12}\mathchar 59\relax\tau_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax-\sigma_{1}^{k})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.53)

where
\mu
contains at least
n-1
differentials of
\sigma_{i}
, one differential of
\rho
and one differential of
\beta_{1(2)}
. There is no restriction on the degree of this form with respect to
\mathrm{d}\tau_{i}
. In the proof, it will be shown how objects with index 1 and 2 can be swapped.

To prove (
2.53
), we first consider the simple case where
\mu
contains the differential
\mathrm{d}\tau_{1(2)}
. Taking into account all other differentials in
\mu
, we observe that on the
r.h.s.

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\cong\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}(\dots)_{\alpha}
,
where the weak equality
\cong
implies equality up to terms, that do not contribute to the integral.
Thus, in the presence of
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{2})
, we have

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\cong\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}(\dots)_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12\beta}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.54)

which vanishes due to the Schouten identity
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{\beta}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{\gamma}=0
. The
l.h.s.
weakly vanishes for the same reason, reducing (
2.53
) to the identity
0=0
.

In the absence of
\mathrm{d}\tau_{1(2)}
in
\mu
, we decompose
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}
on the
r.h.s.
as follows:

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\cong 2\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}+p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})+s^{\alpha}(1-\beta_{1})-\beta_{1}v_{1}^{\alpha}+\rho_{1}(y^{\alpha}+p_{+}^{\alpha}+s^{\alpha}+u_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{12}^{\alpha}))\mathrm{d}p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{1}).

(2.55)

Since
t_{\alpha}
does not contribute to the pre-exponential factor integration over this variable gives the delta function
\delta^{2}(s+\Omega_{12}+y+u_{12})
. After integration over
s_{\alpha}
one gets

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\cong 2\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\beta_{1}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-(1-\beta_{1})(y^{\alpha}+u_{12}^{\alpha})+p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})-\beta_{1}v_{1}^{\alpha}+\rho_{1}(p_{+}^{\alpha}-\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}+u_{1}^{\alpha}))\mathrm{d}p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{1}).

(2.56)

The exponent acquires the form

\mathcal{E}=\exp i\big((\Omega_{12}-(1-\tau_{1})(\beta_{1}\Omega_{12}-(1-\beta_{1})(y+u_{12})+p(\sigma_{1})-\beta_{1}v_{1}))_{\alpha}(-\Omega_{12}+p_{+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+\\
+{\Omega}_{12\alpha}(y-p_{+}+u_{2}+u_{12})^{\alpha}-\Omega_{12\alpha}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+p_{+{\alpha}}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}+u_{12\alpha}v_{12}^{\alpha}+\\
-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big)=\exp i\big((-(1-\tau_{1})(\beta_{1}\Omega_{12}-(1-\beta_{1})(y+u_{12})+p(\sigma_{1})-\beta_{1}v_{1}))_{\alpha}(-\Omega_{12}+\\
+p_{+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+{\Omega}_{12\alpha}(u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{12})^{\alpha}+p_{+{\alpha}}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}+u_{12\alpha}v_{12}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big).

(2.57)

Analogously, on the
l.h.s.
of (
2.53
) one integrates over
s_{\alpha}
to get
\delta^{2}(t-\Omega_{12}+y+u_{12})
. Then integration over
t_{\alpha}
yields

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\cong 2\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}(\beta_{2}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}+(1-\beta_{2})(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})-\beta_{2}v_{2}^{\alpha}+\rho_{2}(-p_{+}^{\alpha}+\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}+u_{2}^{\alpha}))\mathrm{d}p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})=\\
=-2\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}(-\beta_{2}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-(1-\beta_{2})(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}-p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})+\beta_{2}v_{2}^{\alpha}+\rho_{2}(p_{+}^{\alpha}-\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-u_{2}^{\alpha}))\mathrm{d}p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})

(2.58)

and

\mathcal{E}=\exp i\big((\Omega_{12}-(1-\tau_{2})(\beta_{2}\Omega_{12}+(1-\beta_{2})(y+u_{12})+p(\sigma_{2})-\beta_{2}v_{2}))_{\alpha}(\Omega_{12}-p_{+}+u_{2})^{\alpha}+{\Omega}_{12\alpha}(y+\\
+p_{+}+u_{1}+u_{12})^{\alpha}-\Omega_{12\alpha}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+p_{+{\alpha}}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}+u_{12\alpha}v_{12}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big)=\\
=\exp i\big((-(1-\tau_{2})(-\beta_{2}\Omega_{12}-(1-\beta_{2})(y+u_{12})-p(\sigma_{2})+\beta_{1}v_{2}))_{\alpha}(-\Omega_{12}+p_{+}-u_{2})^{\alpha}+\\
+{\Omega}_{12\alpha}(u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{12})^{\alpha}+p_{+{\alpha}}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}+u_{12\alpha}v_{12}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big).

(2.59)

Changing a sign of
u_{2\alpha}
and
v_{2\alpha}
to
-v_{2\alpha}+2\Omega_{12\alpha}
, one obtains

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\cong-2\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}(\beta_{2}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-(1-\beta_{2})(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}-p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})-\beta_{2}v_{2}^{\alpha}+\rho_{2}(p_{+}^{\alpha}-\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}+u_{2}^{\alpha}))\mathrm{d}p_{\alpha}(\sigma_{2})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.60)

\mathcal{E}=\exp i\big[(-(1-\tau_{2})(\beta_{2}\Omega_{12}-(1-\beta_{2})(y+u_{12})-p(\sigma_{2})-\beta_{1}v_{2}))_{\alpha}(-\Omega_{12}+p_{+}+u_{2})^{\alpha}+{\Omega}_{12\alpha}(u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{12})^{\alpha}+\\
+p_{+{\alpha}}(y+u_{12})^{\alpha}+u_{1\alpha}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2\alpha}v_{2}^{\alpha}++u_{12\alpha}v_{12}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i<j}p_{i\alpha}p_{j}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i\alpha}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big].

(2.61)

Interchange of the indices 1 and 2 completes the proof (exchange of
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
, change of a sign of
\sigma_{2}
, together with the minus from (
2.58
) gives a minus on the
r.h.s.
of (
2.53
)).

Analogous property holds true in the presence of a factor of
\mathbb{D}(\tau_{i})\mathbb{D}(a_{i})

\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(a_{2})\mathbb{D}(a_{1}-(1-\beta_{1}))\mathbb{D}(\tau_{1})\mu(\tau_{12}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{12}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\tau_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{2}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{k})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})=\\
=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}}\tau_{j}\rho_{i}\beta_{i}\sigma_{j}^{k}a_{i}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(a_{1})\mathbb{D}(a_{2}-(1-\beta_{2}))\mathbb{D}(\tau_{2})\mu(\tau_{12}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{12}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\tau_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2}\mathchar 59\relax-\sigma_{2}^{k})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}g_{1}(r_{1})\bar{*}\dots\bar{*}g_{n}(r_{n})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.62)

where
\mu
contains
n-2
differentials of
\sigma_{1}
on the
l.h.s.
of the equality.

The proof of (
2.62
) is analogous to that of (
2.53
). Namely, on the
l.h.s.
one should integrate over
t_{\alpha}
, that yields
\delta^{2}(s+\Omega_{2}+y+u_{12})
integrated over
s_{\alpha}
. On the
r.h.s.
the integral over
s_{\alpha}
gives
\delta^{2}(t-\Omega_{1}+y+u_{12})
to be integrated over
t_{\alpha}
. As a result, swapping the indices 1 and 2, one arrives at (
2.62
).

2.6
Realization of the shifted homotopy operator

In
[
27
]
, the
\beta
-shifted contracting homotopy operator

\Delta_{p(\sigma_{0})\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{0}}F=\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{u^{2}v^{2}}u^{2}v^{2}}$\hss}}}d^{2}ud^{2}v\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau}\tau}$\hss}}}\dfrac{d\tau}{\tau}l(\tau)(z+p(\sigma_{0})-\beta_{0}v)^{\alpha}\dfrac{1}{\partial\theta^{\alpha}}F(\Omega_{0}\mathchar 59\relax y+u|\tau\theta)\exp iu_{\gamma}v^{\gamma}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.63)

with

l(\tau)=\theta(1-\tau)\theta(\tau)\mathchar 59\relax

(2.64)

\Omega_{0}=\tau z-(1-\tau)\left[p(\sigma_{0})-\beta_{0}v\right]

(2.65)

was introduced to obtain a quadratic ultralocal vertex in the one-form holomorphic sector.

As we show now, the action of this operator can be realized in terms of the differential homotopy approach, demonstrating that the latter is more general.
That the two methods are not fully equivalent follows from the construction of a differential homotopy for solving the
B_{2}
-field equation in
[
34
]
which particular solution admits no shifted homotopy realization.

We define an operator
\vartriangleright_{\lambda}
acting on differential forms in
z_{\alpha}
and auxiliary homotopy variables to generate
a differential form in an extended space with additional variables
\tau\mathchar 59\relax\beta\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{i}

and spinors
u_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax v_{\alpha}
:

\left(\vphantom{\big|}\vartriangleright_{\lambda}F(z\mathchar 59\relax y|\theta)\right)[\tau\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta\mathchar 59\relax u\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}v]=d^{2}u\;d^{2}v\;\lambda(\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta)l(\tau)F(\Omega\mathchar 59\relax y+u|\mathrm{d}\Omega)\exp iu_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

(2.66)

where
\Omega_{\alpha}=\tau z_{\alpha}-(1-\tau)\left[p_{\alpha}(\sigma)-\beta v_{\alpha}\right]
while
\lambda(\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta)
is some closed differential form. To reproduce the standard results obtained by the
\beta
-shifted contracting homotopy technique one can choose the measure
\lambda
in the form

\lambda(\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta)=\lambda_{0}(\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta)=\prod_{i}\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{i}-\sigma_{0}^{i})\cdot\mathbb{D}(\beta-\beta_{0})\mathchar 59\relax

(2.67)

where
\sigma_{0}^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{0}
are constants.
It can be shown that the action of this operator,
with the measure form (
2.67
),
coincides with that of the
\beta
-shifted homotopy operator in a weak sense

\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{u^{2}v^{2}\tau\sigma^{i}\beta}u^{2}v^{2}\tau\sigma^{i}\beta}$\hss}}}\quad\vartriangleright_{\lambda_{0}}F\cong\Delta_{p(\sigma_{0})\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{0}}F\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\text{with }\lambda_{0}(\sigma^{i}\mathchar 59\relax\beta)=\prod_{i}\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{i}-\sigma_{0}^{i})\cdot\mathbb{D}(\beta-\beta_{0}).

(2.68)

Details of the proof of this statement, along with some its useful consequences
are presented in Appendix
B.1
.

3
Application to higher-spin equations

3.1
Generic system

The differential homotopy approach is applied to the system (
1.8
) as follows
[
34
]
. We make the fields depend on the homotopy parameters
h^{a}
and the variables
p_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax r_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax u_{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax v_{\alpha}
along with
Z\mathchar 59\relax Y\mathchar 59\relax x
. The original fields are restored via integration over all additional variables.

It is useful to expand the fields
{B}
and
{W}
in degrees of
differential forms as follows:

\mathcal{B}=B+\sum_{n\geq 1}{B}^{(n)}\mathchar 59\relax\quad{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}=W+\sum_{n\geq 2}W^{(n)}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.1)

where
{B}^{(n)}
and
W^{(n)}
are space-time
n
-forms.
Thus,
\mathcal{B}
contains higher forms of positive degrees.

We also introduce notation

\mathcal{W}={\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}+S-\theta^{A}Z_{A}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.2)

where the vacuum solution (
1.13
) is explicitly taken into account. Then the system (
1.8
) is replaced by

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}_{x}-2i\mathrm{d})\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{W}*\mathcal{B}-\mathcal{B}*\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\cong 0\mathchar 59\relax

(3.3a)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}_{x}-2i\mathrm{d})\mathcal{W}+\mathcal{W}*\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{V}_{2}\cong i\mathcal{B}*(\eta\gamma+\bar{\eta}\bar{\gamma})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(3.3b)

where the weak equality
\cong
implies equality up to terms, that do not contribute to the integral over additional variables. The property (
1.14
) is also taken into account.

Since equations (
3.3
) contain space-time differential forms of different degrees, they split into independent equations for each degree.

Equations (
3.3
) are invariant under the following gauge transformations

\begin{gathered}\delta\mathcal{W}=(\mathrm{d}_{x}-2i\mathrm{d})\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}+[\mathcal{W}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}]_{*}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\delta\mathcal{B}=[\mathcal{B}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}]_{*}\mathchar 59\relax\\
\delta\mathcal{V}_{2}=[\mathcal{V}_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}]_{*}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\delta\mathcal{V}_{1}=[\mathcal{V}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}]_{*}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
\delta\mathcal{B}=(\mathrm{d}_{x}-2i\mathrm{d})\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}+\left\{\mathcal{W}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}\right\}_{*}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\delta\mathcal{V}_{1}=[\mathcal{V}_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}]_{*}\mathchar 59\relax\end{gathered}

(3.4)

where the gauge parameters
\epsilon_{\mathcal{W}}
and
\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}
ar
zero- and one-forms in
\theta
, respectively. (We use notations
[a\mathchar 59\relax b]_{*}=a*b-b*a\mathchar 59\relax\;\{a\mathchar 59\relax b\}_{*}=a*b+b*a
.)

Note that all higher degree forms except for the first ones in the expansions (
3.1
) do not affect the physical equations. For instance, let
B^{(1)}
enter the field
C
equation as follows:

\mathrm{d}_{x}C+\omega*C-C*\omega-2i\mathrm{d}B^{(1)}+\dots=0\,.

(3.5)

then the part with
B^{(1)}
under the differential
\mathrm{d}_{t}
does not contribute upon integration over
M
while that under
\mathrm{d}_{Z}
does not affect the
Z\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\,\theta
-independent sector of the dynamical equations. Analogously, the equation for the one-form field
\omega
takes the form

\mathrm{d}_{x}\omega+\omega*\omega-2i\mathrm{d}W^{(2)}+\dots=0\,.

(3.6)

3.2
Vacuum solution

The vacuum solution is chosen as follows

\mathcal{B}=0\mathchar 59\relax\quad\mathcal{W}=\omega(Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)\mathchar 59\relax

(3.7)

where
\omega(Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K|x)
is a polynomial in
Y_{A}
that verifies the flatness equation

\mathrm{d}_{x}\omega(Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)+\omega(Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)*\omega(Y\mathchar 59\relax K|x)=0.

(3.8)

3.3
First-order calculations

Substitution of the vacuum solution into (
3.3
) yields in the first order

\mathrm{d}_{x}\mathcal{B}_{1}+\omega*\mathcal{B}_{1}-\mathcal{B}_{1}*\omega-2i\mathrm{d}\mathcal{B}_{1}\cong 0\,.

(3.9)

The
\mathrm{d}x
–independent part of this equation implies that the zero-form part of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
(
i.e.

B_{1}
) is
\mathrm{d}
-closed. The gauge transformation in the last line of (
3.4
) allows us to eliminate the exact part setting
\mathcal{B}_{1}=C(Y\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K|x)
. The latter is subject to a condition resulting from the degree one sector in
\mathrm{d}x^{n}
of (
3.9
),

\mathrm{d}_{x}C+\omega*C-C*\omega=0\,.

(3.10)

Equation (
3.3b
) allows us to find
S_{1}
. In the
\bar{\eta}
-independent holomorphic sector, this equation takes the form

-2i\mathrm{d}S_{1}\cong i\eta C*\gamma\,.

(3.11)

Its solution is found in the form
[
34
]

S_{1}=-\dfrac{\eta}{2}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma}\tau\rho\beta\sigma}$\hss}}}l(\tau)\mathbb{D}(\sigma)\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)C(r\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r=0}k

(3.12)

(the
u_{i}^{\alpha}
,
v_{i}^{\alpha}
integrations and measure
\mu_{0}
are implicit), where

\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)=\mu(\beta)\mathbb{D}(\rho)\,\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\qquad\int_{\beta}\mu(\beta)=1\,.

(3.13)

Here and below we use the representation (
2.45
).

Acting by
\mathrm{d}
on the
r.h.s.
of (

) we get two terms. The one with

W_{1}
is determined from the equation

-2i\mathrm{d}W_{1}+\mathrm{d}_{x}S_{1}+[\omega\mathchar 59\relax S_{1}]_{*}\cong 0\,.

The result splits into a sum of two terms corresponding to the two possible orderings of the product factors —

W_{1}|_{\omega C}=\dfrac{i\eta}{4}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma\sigma^{\omega}}\tau\rho\beta\sigma\sigma^{\omega}}$\hss}}}l(\tau)\mathbb{D}(\sigma)P(\beta-1\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma)\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)\omega(r_{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r=0}k\,\mathchar 59\relax

(3.15)

(3.17)

The space-time two-form sector of equation (

) has the form

(3.19)

and

\mathrm{d}_{x}\omega+\omega*\omega=\dfrac{\eta}{4i}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma\sigma^{\omega 1}\sigma^{\omega 2}}\tau\rho\beta\sigma\sigma^{\omega 1}\sigma^{\omega 2}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(\tau)\mathbb{D}(\sigma)\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)\big[\\

(3.20)

Equations (

[

3.4
Second-order calculations

In the second order, calculations start with the equation

2i\mathrm{d}B_{2}\cong[S_{1}\mathchar 59\relax C]_{*}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.21)

that can be solved as follows

B_{2sh}=\dfrac{\eta}{4i}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}}\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}}$\hss}}}l(\tau)P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\overrightarrow{\sigma})\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma})\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r_{i}=0}k

or

B_{2pc}=\dfrac{\eta}{4i}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}}\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}}$\hss}}}l(\tau)P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\overrightarrow{\sigma})\mathbb{D}(\rho+\sigma^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma})(\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}+2i(1-\beta)\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{2})\mathrm{d}\tau)\times\\
\times\mu(\beta)\mathcal{E}(\Omega)C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r_{i}=0}k\,\mathchar 59\relax

(3.23)

where

\overrightarrow{\sigma}:=1-\beta\,.

(3.24)

In

, we show that

reproduces the shift

found in
[
27
]
to reach the vertex with the minimal number of derivatives. This is the new result of the paper. In the sequel we will use

.

Note also that Ansatz (

) is appropriate for the following reasons. The factor of

ensures that the coefficient

, which

[

spin-locality of the corrections to physical vertex. Indeed, the part of the exponent affecting this coefficient at

\exp i\big[-(p(\sigma)-\beta v)_{\alpha}(p_{+}+u)^{\alpha}+u_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}\big].

(3.25)

Upon integration over

and

we obtain that the coefficient in front of

is

\frac{\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{1}}{1-\beta}-1\mathchar 59\relax

(3.26)

that is zero for the measure factor in (
3.23
).

The shift of
\rho
, and, as a result, the appearance of an additional term without
\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}
allows one to achieve projectively-compact quadratic vertex in (
1.16
)
[
34
]
.

Now consider the equation for

\mathrm{d}_{x}C+[\omega\mathchar 59\relax C]_{*}+[W_{1}\mathchar 59\relax C]_{*}+\mathrm{d}_{x}B_{2}+[\omega\mathchar 59\relax B_{2}]_{*}-2i\mathrm{d}B_{2}^{(1)}\cong 0\mathchar 59\relax

(3.27)

where
[

]

B_{2}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{2i}\int_{\sigma^{\omega}}P(\beta-1\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{1})B_{2}(\omega CC).

(3.28)

Substitution of (

J=\dfrac{i\eta}{4}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}}\tau\rho\beta\sigma^{1}\sigma^{2}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(\tau)\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{1}+\rho)\mathbb{D}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma})\mu(\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)\\
\big[P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\overrightarrow{\sigma})\omega(r_{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)+\\

+P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\overrightarrow{\sigma})C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}\omega(r_{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big]\big|_{r_{i}=0}\,.

3.5
\mathbf{S_{2}}

S_{2}
is determined by the equation

Calculation of

S_{1}*S_{1}=\dfrac{\eta^{2}}{4}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}}\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(\tau_{12}-1)l(\tau_{1})l(\tau_{2})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{2}-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{2}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{2}){\mu}(\rho_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{1}){\mu}(\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2})\times\\
\times\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r_{i}=0}.

(3.32)

To calculate

we use that

can be represented in the form

-i\eta\gamma=i\eta\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta}\tau\rho\beta}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(1-\tau)\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)k.

-i\eta B_{2}*\gamma=-\dfrac{\eta^{2}}{4}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}}\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}}$\hss}}}\mathbb{D}(\tau_{12}-1)l(\tau_{1})\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{2})P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{2})\mathbb{D}(\rho_{1}+\sigma_{1}^{1})\times\\
\times{\mu}(\beta_{1}){\mu}(\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2})(\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}+2i(1-\beta_{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{2})\mathrm{d}\tau_{1})\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r_{i}=0}.

(3.34)

S_{2}
can be found in the form

S_{2}=-\dfrac{\eta^{2}}{8i}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}\sigma_{12}^{1}\sigma_{12}^{2}\beta_{12}}\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}\sigma_{12}^{1}\sigma_{12}^{2}\beta_{12}}$\hss}}}\mu^{\prime}(\beta_{12})l(\tau_{12})l(\tau_{1})l(\tau_{2})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{2}-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{2}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{2})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{2})\times\\
\times{\mu}(\rho_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{1}){\mu}(\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2})\mathrm{d}{\Omega}_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}{\Omega}_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r_{i}=0}+\\
+\dfrac{\eta^{2}}{8i}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}\sigma_{12}^{1}\sigma_{12}^{2}\beta_{12}}\tau_{1}\rho_{1}\beta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{1}\sigma_{1}^{2}\tau_{2}\rho_{2}\beta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\tau_{12}\sigma_{12}^{1}\sigma_{12}^{2}\beta_{12}}$\hss}}}\mu^{\prime}(\beta_{12})l(\tau_{12})l(\tau_{1})\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{2})P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{1}-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{2}^{2})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{12}^{2})\times\\
\times\mathbb{D}(\rho_{1}+\sigma_{1}^{1}){\mu}(\beta_{1}){\mu}(\rho_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\beta_{2})(\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}+2i(1-\beta_{1})\mathbb{D}(\sigma_{1}^{2})\mathrm{d}\tau_{1})\mathrm{d}{\Omega}_{2}^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\Omega_{2})C(r_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\bar{*}C(r_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r_{i}=0}

(3.35)

(here
a_{1}=1-\beta_{1}\mathchar 59\relax a_{2}=1-\beta_{2}
,
\int_{\beta_{12}}\mu^{\prime}(\beta_{12})=1
).
This can be checked as follows.

When
\mathrm{d}
acts on (
3.35
), the following terms arise. The terms containing
\mathbb{D}(\tau_{1})
,
\mathbb{D}(\tau_{2})
, and
\mathbb{D}(\tau_{12})
are weak because the degree of the form to be integrated exceeds the dimension of
M
. When
\mathrm{d}
acts on the first term in (
3.35
), the terms with
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{i})
are zero for the following reason. Consider for definiteness
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{2})
. In that case

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{1}^{\alpha}=\tau_{1}\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}-(1-\tau_{1})(p_{2}^{\alpha}(1-\beta_{1})+s^{\alpha}(1-\beta_{1})-\beta_{1}v_{1}^{\alpha}+\rho_{1}(y^{\alpha}+p_{+}^{\alpha}+s^{\alpha}+u_{1}^{\alpha}))\mathchar 59\relax\\
\Omega_{2}^{\alpha}=\Omega_{12}^{\alpha}.\end{gathered}

(3.36)

Consequently

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\propto\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\dots)^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}^{2}=0

(3.37)

since
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\beta}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{\gamma}=0
. The action of
\mathrm{d}
on
P(-\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{1}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})=\theta(\sigma_{1}^{1}+\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}})\theta(\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{1})\theta(\overrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}-\sigma_{1}^{2})
in the second term in (
3.35
) yields (
3.37
) again because
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{1}^{1}
and
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{1}^{2}
are present in the measure and only
\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}
and
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{12}
contribute from
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}
. When acting on the exponent in the term where
\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{2}
is absent, one gets a form degree higher than the dimension of
M
. The term with
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{12})
gives the sum of (
3.32
) and (
3.34
).

Choosing a solution of the form (
3.35
) allows us to obtain spin-local vertex. The novelty compared to the lower-order case of (
3.23
)
(without
\tau_{12}
) is that the physical field equations now retain a contribution from the
\tau_{12}=0
condition, which does not enforce
\tau_{1}=0
and/or
\tau_{2}=0
. As a result, the coefficient in front of
p_{1\alpha}p_{2}^{\alpha}
in the exponent responsible for locality may be different from zero. However, as shown below, it still can be eliminated in the appropriate limit.

3.6
\mathbf{W_{2}}

W_{2}
can be obtained from the equation

2i\mathrm{d}W_{2}\cong\mathrm{d}_{x}S_{1}+\mathrm{d}_{x}S_{2}+\omega*S_{2}+S_{2}*\omega+W_{1}*S_{1}+S_{1}*W_{1}-i\eta B_{2}^{(2)}*\gamma.

(3.38)

The last term here is weakly zero since
B_{2}^{(2)}\cong 0
. On the other hand,
\mathrm{d}_{x}S_{1}
is accounted since the action of

on
C
contained in

(
3.12
) yields (

) with

contributing to the second order,

\mathrm{d}_{x}S_{1}|_{CC\omega\mathchar 59\relax C\omega C\mathchar 59\relax\omega CC}=\dfrac{\eta}{2}\int\limits_{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\hphantom{\tau\rho\beta\sigma}\tau\rho\beta\sigma}$\hss}}}l(\tau)\mathbb{D}(\sigma)\mu(\rho\mathchar 59\relax\beta)\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\mathcal{E}(\Omega)J(r\mathchar 59\relax\bar{y}\mathchar 59\relax K)\big|_{r=0}k\,.

(3.39)

Consider the
CC\omega
sector. Let us rewrite the expression (

) in the form (

). We use the convention that the lower index 1 denotes objects related to

, while 2 refers to

\exp i\big[\Omega_{\alpha}(y+t+u_{1})^{\alpha}+\Omega_{J\alpha}(s+p_{+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+\\
+u_{1{\alpha}}v_{1}^{\alpha}+u_{2{\alpha}}v_{2}^{\alpha}-p_{1{\alpha}}p_{2}^{\alpha}-p_{1{\alpha}}p_{\omega}^{\alpha}-p_{2{\alpha}}p_{\omega}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}p_{i{\alpha}}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big].

(3.40)

Shifting
s_{\alpha}\rightarrow s_{\alpha}+y_{\alpha}
and
t_{\alpha}\rightarrow t_{\alpha}-p_{+\alpha}
we obtain

\exp i\big[\Omega_{\alpha}(y+t-p_{+}+u_{2})^{\alpha}+(\Omega_{J\alpha}-s_{\alpha})(y+s+p_{+}+u_{1})^{\alpha}+s_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+(s_{\alpha}-t_{\alpha})y^{\alpha}+p_{+\alpha}y^{\alpha}+u_{1{\alpha}}(v_{1}-s)^{\alpha}+\\
+u_{2{\alpha}}v_{2}^{\alpha}-p_{1{\alpha}}p_{2}^{\alpha}-p_{1{\alpha}}p_{\omega}^{\alpha}-p_{2{\alpha}}p_{\omega}^{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}p_{i{\alpha}}r_{i}^{\alpha}\big].

(3.41)

Changing

to

and taking into account the factor of
\mathbb{D}(\tau_{1})
in the expression for

(
3.30

\Omega_{J}^{\alpha}-s^{\alpha}=-(p^{\alpha}(\sigma_{1})-\beta_{1}(v_{1}^{\alpha}+s^{\alpha})+\rho_{1}(y^{\alpha}+p_{+}^{\alpha}+s^{\alpha}+u_{1}^{\alpha}))-s^{\alpha}=\Omega_{1}^{\alpha}(\tau_{1}=0).

(3.42)

Next, replacing
\tau
by
\tau_{2}
and adding integration over
\tau_{12}
with
\mathbb{D}(1-\tau_{12})
we bring
\Omega
to the form of
